Measurements of the Higgs boson production, fiducial and differential cross-sections in the four lepton decay channel using 164 fb -1 of data collected at s \sqrt{s} = 13.6 TeV with the ATLAS detector


Abstract
Inclusive, differential and production-mode cross-section measurements of the Higgs boson are performed in the H → Z ​ Z ∗ → 4 ​ ℓ H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell decay channel. The analysis uses proton–proton collision data produced at the Large Hadron Collider at a centre-of-mass energy of s = 13.6 \sqrt{s}=13.6 TeV and recorded with the ATLAS detector, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 164 fb -1 . The inclusive fiducial cross-section for the H → Z ​ Z ∗ → 4 ​ ℓ H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell process is measured to be σ fid = 3.65 − 0.33 + 0.35 ​ fb \sigma_{\textrm{fid}}=3.65^{+0.35}_{-0.33}\penalty\ \textrm{fb} , in agreement with the Standard Model prediction of σ fid SM = 3.68 ± 0.17 ​ fb \sigma_{\textrm{fid}}^{\textrm{SM}}=3.68\pm 0.17\penalty\ \textrm{fb} . Differential cross-sections are measured as a function of key kinematic observables of the Higgs boson and the four-lepton final state. Cross-sections are measured for the main production-modes in several exclusive regions of the Higgs boson production phase space and combined to measure an overall Higgs boson signal strength, defined as the measured cross-section normalised to the SM prediction, of μ = 0.99 ± 0.13 \mu=0.99\pm 0.13 . The results are interpreted in terms of modifications of Higgs boson couplings using the κ \kappa framework, within the Standard Model Effective Field Theory, and in scenarios probing the Higgs boson self-coupling. All the results are consistent with Standard Model expectations.

HIGP-2025-09

CERN-EP-2026-112

1
Introduction

The observation of the Higgs boson by the ATLAS and CMS Collaborations
[
1
,
2
]
using proton–proton (
pp
) collisions at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) marked a major milestone in the understanding of the electroweak (EW) symmetry-breaking mechanism
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
. Subsequent studies of the Higgs boson properties have established the observed particle as a scalar boson consistent with the Standard Model (SM) predictions for a Higgs boson with a mass of
125.09\pm 0.24\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}

[
6
]
.

The
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
decay channel, where
\ell=e,\mu
, provides a particularly clear experimental signature, featuring an excellent signal-to-background ratio and full reconstruction of the final state enabled by the high-precision lepton momentum measurements of the ATLAS detector. Background contributions are small and can be precisely estimated by using data-driven techniques. As a result, this channel has played a central role in precision measurements of Higgs boson properties by both the ATLAS and CMS experiments using the full LHC Run 2 integrated luminosity of
140\penalty\ \mathrm{fb}^{-1}
. At a centre-of-mass energy of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
, measurements of the Higgs boson production and differential cross-sections
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
12
]
, and derived constraints on couplings, as well as its mass
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
, tensor structure
[
17
,
18
]
, and width
[
19
,
16
]
, have been performed. More recently, first measurements at
\sqrt{s}=13.6\penalty\ \text{TeV}
have been reported by ATLAS
[
20
]
and CMS
[
21
]
.

This paper presents measurements of Higgs boson production using
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
decay candidates recorded during Run 3 with the ATLAS experiment in
pp
collisions at
\sqrt{s}=13.6\penalty\ \text{TeV}
between the 2022 and 2024 data-taking periods, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of
$164\text{\,}\mathrm{f}\mathrm{b}$^{-1}
. Inclusive and differential cross-sections for eight observables sensitive to the production and decay kinematics of the Higgs boson, are measured within a well-defined fiducial phase space. In addition, cross-sections per Higgs boson production-mode are extracted using the Simplified Template cross-section (STXS) framework
[
22
,
23
]
.

The cross-sections are determined using binned likelihood fits to the reconstructed four-lepton invariant mass spectrum for the inclusive and differential cross-sections measurement, and are corrected for detector efficiencies and resolution effects through an unfolding procedure. Neural-network-based (NN) discriminant distributions are used for the production-mode cross-sections measurements.

The measured cross-sections are compared with SM predictions and are further interpreted to constrain possible anomalous Higgs boson couplings. In particular, the differential production cross-sections are interpreted in the context of the Standard Model effective field theory (SMEFT), while the cross-sections per production-mode are interpreted in terms of modified Higgs boson couplings to vector bosons. Constraints on the Higgs boson trilinear self-coupling are derived by exploiting both the Higgs boson transverse momentum spectra and the production-mode cross-sections. Furthermore, a statistical combination of Run 2 and Run 3 data is performed in the context of the coupling-modifier interpretation, significantly improving the precision of the constraints.

Compared with previous results in this analysis channel
[
8
,
9
,
20
]
, the results presented in this paper benefit from additional data collected in 2023 and 2024. A new transformer-based
[
24
]
machine learning (ML) algorithm is used to identify jets from the hadronisation of b-quarks, providing substantial improvements in background rejection
[
25
]
for measuring the top-quark-associated Higgs production-mode. A data-driven fake-factor method
[
26
]
is used to estimate backgrounds with non-prompt leptons, such as those arising from
Z
+jets,
t\bar{t}
, and
WZ
processes. A new double-differential distribution of the transverse momentum of the four-lepton system as function of the invariant mass of the sub-leading dilepton pair is measured for the first time, owing to its high sensitivity to possible beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) effects in Higgs boson couplings. A new neural-network architecture is also employed to construct discriminants that enhance the sensitivity to distinguish between the various Higgs boson production-modes. In addition, the azimuthal angle between the decay planes of the two
Z
bosons,
\phi
, was found to improve sensitivity to SM-BSM interference effects and is reinterpreted in the context of SMEFT for the first time in this channel.

The paper is organised as follows. Section
2
provides a brief description of the ATLAS detector. The data used in the analysis, together with the simulated signal and background samples, is described in Section
3
. The event selection for Higgs boson candidates is presented in Section
4
, while the estimation of background contributions is discussed in Section
5
. An overview of the analysis methodology used to measure inclusive and differential cross-sections in a fiducial phase space, and production-mode cross-sections in the STXS framework, is given in Section
6
. The experimental and theoretical systematic uncertainties affecting the measurements are summarised in Section
7
. The cross-section results are presented in Section
8
, and their interpretation is discussed in Section
9
. Finally, the conclusions are given in Section
10
.

2
ATLAS detector

The ATLAS experiment
[
27
,
28
]
at the LHC is a multipurpose particle detector
with a forward–backward symmetric cylindrical geometry and a near
4\pi
coverage in
solid angle.
1
1

1

ATLAS uses a right-handed coordinate system with its origin at the nominal interaction point (IP)
in the centre of the detector and the
z
-axis along the beam pipe.
The
x
-axis points from the IP to the centre of the LHC ring,
and the
y
-axis points upwards.
Polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
are used in the transverse plane,

\phi
being the azimuthal angle around the
z
-axis.
The pseudorapidity is defined in terms of the polar angle
\theta
as
\eta=-\ln\tan(\theta/2)
and is equal to the rapidity

y=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}\right)
in the relativistic limit.
Angular distance is measured in units of
\Delta R\equiv\sqrt{(\Delta y)^{2}+(\Delta\phi)^{2}}
.

It consists of an inner tracking detector (ID) surrounded by a thin superconducting solenoid
providing a
2\text{\,}\mathrm{T}
axial magnetic field, electromagnetic and hadronic calorimeters, and a muon spectrometer.
The inner tracking detector covers the pseudorapidity range
|\eta|<2.5
.
It consists of silicon pixel, silicon microstrip, and transition radiation tracking (TRT) detectors.
Lead/liquid-argon (LAr) sampling calorimeters provide electromagnetic (EM) energy measurements
with high granularity within the region
|\eta|<3.2
.
A steel/scintillator-tile hadronic calorimeter covers the central pseudorapidity range (
|\eta|<1.7
).
The endcap and forward regions are instrumented with LAr calorimeters
for EM and hadronic energy measurements up to
|\eta|=4.9
.
The muon spectrometer (MS) comprises separate trigger and
high-precision tracking chambers measuring the deflection of muons in a magnetic field generated by the superconducting air-core toroidal magnets.
The field integral of the toroids ranges between
2.0
and
6.0\text{\,}\mathrm{T}\text{\,}\mathrm{m}

across most of the detector.
Three layers of precision chambers, each consisting of layers of monitored drift tubes, cover the region
|\eta|<2.7
,
except in the innermost layer of the end-cap region, where layers of small-strip thin-gap chambers and Micromegas chambers both provide precision tracking in the region
1.3<|\eta|<2.7
.
The muon trigger system covers the range
|\eta|<2.4
with resistive-plate chambers in the barrel, thin-gap chambers in the endcap regions, and the aforementioned small-strip thin-gap chambers and Micromegas chambers in the innermost layer of the endcap.
The luminosity is measured mainly by the LUCID–2 detector which is located close to the beampipe.
A two-level trigger system is used to select events
[
29
]
.
The first-level trigger is implemented in hardware and uses a subset of the detector information
to accept events at a rate close to
100\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
.
This is followed by a software-based trigger that
reduces the accepted rate of complete events to
3\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
on average,
depending on the data-taking conditions.
A software suite
[
30
]
is used in data simulation, in the reconstruction
and analysis of real and simulated data, in detector operations, and in the trigger and data acquisition
systems of the experiment.

3
Signal and background simulation

3.1
Data

The measurements presented in this paper are performed using
pp
collision data recorded with the ATLAS detector between 2022 and 2024, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of
$164\text{\,}\mathrm{f}\mathrm{b}$^{-1}
. Data are selected only if all detector components relevant for each analysis channel are in good operating condition. The uncertainty in the integrated luminosity is 1.9%
[
31
]
, following the methodology described in Ref.
[
32
]
.
The average number of interactions per bunch crossing (pile-up), averaged over all colliding bunch pairs, is about 42 in 2022, increases to about 51 in 2023, and reaches approximately 57 in 2024.

Events used in the measurement are recorded using a combination of single-lepton, dilepton, and trilepton triggers
[
33
,
34
]
. The electron triggers used for the 2023 and 2024 data-taking periods exploit the new electromagnetic feature extractors deployed for LHC Run 3
[
29
]
. The transverse momentum thresholds of the single-lepton triggers are 24
GeV
for muons and 26
GeV
for electrons. The dielectron triggers require a minimum transverse momentum of 17
GeV
per electron, while the dimuon triggers require at least 14
GeV
per muon. Mixed lepton flavour
e\mu
triggers combine these thresholds, and trilepton triggers allow for lower thresholds in exchange for a higher lepton multiplicity.
For simulated Higgs boson candidates produced via the gluon–gluon fusion process, the resulting total trigger efficiency is 98.1% for signal events satisfying the final selection described in Section
4
.

3.2
Simulated event samples

This analysis uses simulated event samples to model the Higgs boson signal and all relevant background processes, and to derive corrections for detector effects.

3.2.1
Signal modelling

Higgs boson production and decay are simulated for the primary production-modes: gluon–gluon fusion (
\mathrm{ggF}
), vector-boson fusion (
\mathrm{VBF}
), associated production with a vector boson (
VH
, where
V=W,Z
), top- or bottom-associated production
2
2

2

t\bar{t}H
and
b\bar{b}H
are also denoted by
ttH
and
bbH
in the figures.
(
t\bar{t}H
and
b\bar{b}H
), and associated production with a single top quark (
t
-channel for
tH
and
tHW
)
3
3

3

The
t
-channel processes
tH
and
tHW
are denoted by
tH
in the figures.
. Hard-scattering matrix-elements (MEs) for the nominal signal samples are generated with the
Powheg Box
v2
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
Monte Carlo (MC) generator. Next-to-leading-order (NLO) accuracy in quantum chromodynamics (QCD) is achieved for the
\mathrm{ggF}
and
VH
processes with up to one additional parton by employing the
MiNLO

[
38
,
39
,
40
]
approach. In a subsequent step, the
\mathrm{ggF}
simulation is improved to next-to-next-to-leading-order (NNLO)
[
41
,
42
]
accuracy for inclusive observables by reweighting the Higgs boson rapidity spectrum in
Hj
-
MiNLO

[
38
,
39
,
40
]
to that obtained with HNNLO
[
43
]
. The
\mathrm{VBF}
and
t\bar{t}H
processes are simulated at NLO accuracy in QCD, while the
b\bar{b}H
sample is generated in the four-flavour scheme. The loop-induced
gg\to ZH
process is generated at leading-order in QCD.
tH
and
tHW
processes are generated at NLO using the
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
generator
[
44
]
. All signal samples are generated assuming a Higgs boson mass of 125
GeV
4
4

4

A shift of 90
MeV
is applied to the signal parameterisation used in the fit to the data to match the measured Higgs boson mass of
125.09\pm 0.24\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}

[
6
]
.
and a Higgs boson width of 4.07
MeV
.

The signal events are then interfaced to a parton-shower generator for showering, hadronisation, modelling of the underlying event and decay of the Higgs boson. All signal samples use the
PDF4LHC21
Parton Density Function (PDF) set
[
45
]
for the ME calculations. The nominal signal samples are interfaced to
Pythia
8.309 to simulate the
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
decays and use the A14 tune for the parton shower
[
46
]
with the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set
[
47
]
.

Alternative signal samples are produced to assess uncertainties related to parton-shower and hadronisation modelling. These are obtained by interfacing the same
Powheg
events to
Herwig
7
[
48
]
with the H7.1 event tune, using the same PDF setup for the ME calculation. In addition, a simulation of the
\mathrm{ggF}
process is generated using the
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
generator and showered with
Pythia
8.312
[
49
]
using the A14 tune with the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set. This sample is accurate to NLO in QCD for up to two additional partons merged using the
FxFx

[
50
,
44
]
merging scheme.

The decays of bottom and charm hadrons are simulated using
EvtGen

[
51
]
.

3.2.2
Signal normalisation and branching ratios

The signal samples are normalised to state-of-the-art theoretical cross-section predictions
[
52
]
interpolated to a centre-of-mass energy of 13.6
TeV

[
53
]
. The
\mathrm{ggF}
sample is normalised to a N
3
LO QCD calculation including NLO EW corrections
[
54
,
55

57
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
]
. The
\mathrm{VBF}
and
VH
samples are normalised to an approximate NNLO QCD cross-section with NLO EW corrections
[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
]
.
The
b\bar{b}H
contribution is normalised using a calculation combines NLO contributions with the resummation of collinear logarithms up to NNLO
[
77
,
78
]
, and are cross-checked using an alternative matching scheme
[
79
,
80
]
.
The
t\bar{t}H
cross-section is taken from a calculation accurate to NLO in QCD with NLO EW corrections
[
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
]
.
tH
and
tHW
are normalized to cross-sections that are accurate to NLO in QCD
[
85
,
86
]
.

The Standard Model prediction for the
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell\penalty\ (\ell=e,\mu)
branching ratio is obtained from
Prophecy4f

[
87
,
88
]
. This prediction includes the full NLO EW corrections and interference effects that lead to a branching ratio approximately 10% higher for same-flavour final states (
4\mu
and
4e
) than for different-flavour final states (
2e2\mu
and
2\mu 2e
). The predicted SM branching ratio is
(1.24\pm 0.03)\times 10^{-4}
for a Higgs boson mass of 125.09
GeV
.

3.2.3
Background modelling

The dominant background contribution arises from continuum
(Z^{(*)}/\gamma^{*})(Z^{(*)}/\gamma^{*})
production, and is denoted by
ZZ^{*}
. The quark-initiated process (
qqZZ^{*}
) is the dominant component, while the corresponding gluon-initiated process (
ggZZ^{*}
) provides a smaller contribution of the order of 10%. Additional background contributions arise from events with non-prompt or misidentified leptons, including those from
Z
boson production in association with jets (
Z
+jets), top-quark pair-production (
t\bar{t}
), and
WZ
diboson production. Additional minor backgrounds include
t\bar{t}+\ell\ell
and triboson (
WWZ
,
WZZ
,
ZZZ
) production.

Continuum
ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
production.

The nominal
qqZZ^{*}
sample is simulated using the
Sherpa
2.2.14
[
89
]
generator, including off-shell effects. Matrix elements for fully leptonic final states are calculated at NLO accuracy in QCD for up to one additional parton and at LO accuracy for up to three additional partons. These matrix elements are matched and merged with the
Sherpa
parton shower, based on Catani–Seymour dipole factorisation
[
90
,
91
]
, using the MEPS@NLO prescription
[
92
,
93
,
94
,
95
]
. Virtual QCD corrections are provided by the
OpenLoops
library
[
96
,
97
,
98
]
. The
NNPDF3.0nnlo
PDF set
[
99
]
is used, together with the dedicated tune.

An alternative
qqZZ^{*}
sample is simulated using the
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
generator with the
NNPDF3.0nnlo
, accurate to NLO in QCD for zero and one additional parton merged using the
FxFx
scheme. In this case, parton showering, hadronisation, and underlying-event modelling are performed with
Pythia
8.308 using the A14 tune with the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set.

The loop-induced
ggZZ^{*}
contribution is simulated at NLO accuracy in QCD matched to a parton shower using the
Powheg
approach
[
100
]
with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set. The NLO calculation includes two-loop virtual corrections and loop-squared real contributions, with heavy-quark mass effects included in the virtual corrections. The real matrix elements retain the full top-quark mass dependence
[
100
]
.

Production of
ZZ^{*}
via electroweak vector-boson-scattering-like topologies is simulated at LO using
Sherpa
2.2.14 with
NNPDF3.0nnlo
PDF, with Higgs-mediated contributions removed at generation level.

Since the overall normalisation of the
ZZ^{*}
background is derived from collision data, the MC predicted cross-sections are used to only determine the relative fraction of the three different processes.

top-quark-associated and triboson backgrounds.

Minor background contributions include top-quark-associated multilepton production and triboson processes. The production of
tWZ
,
tZq
,
t\bar{t}Z
, and
t\bar{t}VV
is modelled using the
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO

[
44
]
generator at NLO accuracy with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set. The events are interfaced to
Pythia
8.230 using the A14 tune and the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set. The
t\bar{t}W
process is simulated using
Sherpa
2.2.14 with
NNPDF3.0nnlo
PDF. The combination of these process is referred to as
tXX
.
Triboson processes (
WWW
,
WWZ
,
WZZ
,
ZZZ
) are simulated using
Sherpa
2.2.16, with matrix elements evaluated at NLO accuracy in QCD for up to one additional jet and at LO accuracy for up to three additional jets, merged with the
Sherpa
parton shower.

Samples for non-prompt lepton background estimates.

Simulated samples of
Z
+jets,
t\bar{t}
, and
WZ
production are used exclusively to support the data-driven estimate of backgrounds arising from non-prompt or misidentified leptons. The
Z
+jets samples are generated with
Sherpa
2.2.14 following the setup described in Ref
[
101
]
. These samples use the
NNPDF3.0nnlo
PDF set and are normalised to a NNLO QCD prediction
[
102
]
.

The
t\bar{t}
process is simulated using the
Powheg Box
v2 generator at NLO accuracy with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set and an
h_{\text{damp}}

5
5

5

The
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter is a resummation damping factor and one of the parameters that controls the matching of
Powheg
matrix elements to the parton shower and thus effectively regulates the high-
p_{\text{T}}
radiation against which the
t\bar{t}
system recoils.
parameter set to
1.5\,m_{\text{top}}

[
103
]
. Events are interfaced to
Pythia
8.230 using the A14 tune and the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set. The decays of bottom and charm hadrons are simulated using
EvtGen
2.1.1
[
51
]
. The
WZ
process is modelled using the
Sherpa
2.2.16 generator with the same setup as used for the
qqZZ^{*}
sample.

3.2.4
Detector simulation and pile-up modelling

All generated signal and background events are passed through a full simulation of the ATLAS detector based on
Geant4

[
104
,
105
]
. Scale factors are applied to the simulation to match the performance of algorithms used to reconstruct physics objects to the one observed in data.

The effects of multiple interactions in the same and neighbouring bunch crossings (pile-up) are modelled by overlaying simulated hard-scattering events with inelastic
pp
collisions generated using a mixture of
Epos
2.0.1.4
[
106
]
and
Pythia
8.308
[
49
]
. The
Epos
events are generated with the EPOS LHC tune
[
107
]
, while the
Pythia
events use the A3 tune
[
108
]
and the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set. The
Pythia
pile-up events include either a high-
p_{\text{T}}
jet, a prompt photon, or a lepton from a
b
-hadron decay, whereas
Epos
is used to model the remaining pile-up activity. The individual samples are first reweighted to ensure a smooth transition across jet
p_{\text{T}}
, and the combined pile-up distribution is subsequently reweighted to match the distribution of the average number of interactions per bunch crossing observed in data.

4
Event selection

Higgs boson candidates are formed by selecting quadruplets of reconstructed and identified charged leptons (electrons and muons). The selection procedure follows that of Ref.
[
20
]
and is briefly summarised below.

4.1
Event reconstruction

Event reconstruction begins with the formation of tracks in the ID
[
109
]
from measurements in the silicon detectors and the TRT, and in the MS from measurements in the muon chambers. ID tracks are reconstructed for
p_{\text{T}}>500\penalty\ \text{MeV}
and are used to form vertex candidates along the beam line
[
110
,
111
]
. Candidate events are required to contain at least one vertex reconstructed from at least two ID tracks.
Among all reconstructed vertices in an event, the primary interaction vertex is defined as the one with the largest summed
p_{\text{T}}^{2}
of its associated tracks.

Electron candidates are reconstructed by matching electromagnetic calorimeter clusters to ID tracks
[
112
]
. A refit of the ID track, allowing for bremsstrahlung energy losses, is performed to improve the impact-parameter
6
6

6

The transverse impact parameter
d_{0}
of a charged-particle track is defined in the transverse plane as the distance of closest approach to the beam spot. The longitudinal impact parameter
z_{0}
is defined as the distance along the beam axis between this point and the primary vertex.
resolution. Electron identification is based on a likelihood discriminant
[
113
]
, using
loose
criteria that combine track properties, electromagnetic shower-shape variables, and the quality of the track–cluster matching. Electrons are required to satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>7\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
|\eta|<2.47
. The electron energy is calibrated following the procedure described in Ref.
[
112
]
. Photons are reconstructed from EM calorimeter signals exceeding noise
thresholds using dynamic topological clustering, with additional clusters included for photon conversion
products when applicable
[
112
]
. The photon candidates are calibrated
[
114
]
and identified by an algorithm that relies on the shape of the EM showers reconstructed in the calorimeter
[
113
]
.

Muon candidates are reconstructed in the pseudorapidity range
|\eta|<2.5
by matching tracks reconstructed in the MS to ID tracks. In the region
|\eta|<0.1
, where the MS is only partially instrumented, muons are additionally reconstructed from ID tracks matched either to partial MS track segments or to calorimeter energy deposits consistent with a minimally ionising particle (calorimeter-tagged muons)
[
115
]
.
Muons are required to have
p_{\text{T}}>5\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, except for calorimeter-tagged muons, which must satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>15\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. A set of
Loose
identification criteria is applied to suppress muons from hadron decays and misidentified tracks
[
115
]
. The muon momentum is calibrated using the procedure described in Ref.
[
116
]
.

Electron and muon candidates are associated with the primary vertex using their impact parameters.
Muon candidates with
|d_{0}|>1\penalty\ \text{mm}
are rejected to suppress cosmic-ray background. Both electron and muon candidates are required to satisfy
|z_{0}\sin\theta|<0.5\penalty\ \text{mm}
.

Jet candidates are reconstructed using the particle-flow (PFlow) algorithm
[
117
]
, which combines calorimeter energy deposits and inner-detector tracks into particle-flow objects that are clustered into jets using the using the anti-
k_{t}

[
118
]
jet algorithm with radius parameter
R=0.4
. Jets are calibrated using simulation with corrections obtained by using
in situ
techniques in data
[
119
]
.
After calibration, jets are required to satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>30\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
|\eta|<4.5
. To suppress jets originating from pile-up interactions, a neural-network-based algorithm
[
120
,
121
]
is used to associate jets with
p_{\text{T}}<60\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
to the primary vertex.

Jets containing
b
-hadrons are identified (
b
-tagged) using the GN2 transformer-based neural network algorithm
[
25
]
. Compared with previous algorithms, GN2 does not rely on lower-level flavour-tagging algorithms and uses a single training to optimise all input features. It demonstrates improved background rejection at a given efficiency compared with the previous tagger
[
25
]
. Two
b
-tagging working points are used, one with a b-tagging efficiency of approximately
85\%
and a tighter working point with an efficiency of about
65\%
.

Ambiguities are resolved if electron, muon, or jet candidates overlap in geometry or share the same detector information.
If the two calorimeter energy clusters from the two electron candidates overlap, the electron with the higher
p_{\text{T}}
is retained. If a reconstructed electron and muon share the same ID track, the muon is rejected if it is calorimeter-tagged; otherwise the electron is rejected. Reconstructed jets geometrically overlapping in a cone of radial size
\Delta R
= 0.1 (0.2) with a muon (an electron) are also removed.

The missing transverse momentum vector,
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, is defined as negative vector sum of the transverse momenta of all the identified and calibrated leptons and jets and the remaining unclustered energy, where the latter is estimated from low-
p_{\text{T}}
tracks associated with the primary vertex but not assigned to any lepton, photon, hadronically decaying
\tau
-lepton or jet candidate
[
122
]
. The missing transverse momentum (
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
) is defined as the magnitude of
\vec{p}_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
.

4.2
Higgs boson candidate formation

Higgs boson candidates are formed from pairs of same-flavour, opposite-charge (SFOC) electron or muon pairs. Each such combination, referred to as a quadruplet, is required to include the leptons that triggered the event, as described in Section
3.1
. The three highest-
p_{\text{T}}
leptons in the quadruplet are required to satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>20
, 15, and 10
GeV
, respectively.

Further requirements are imposed on the invariant masses of the SFOC lepton pairs. For each quadruplet, the lepton pair with invariant mass
m_{12}
closest to the
Z
-boson mass
[
123
]
is defined as the leading pair, while the remaining SFOC pair, with invariant mass
m_{34}
second-closest to the
Z
-boson mass, is defined as the subleading pair. On the selected SFOC lepton pairs, additional requirements are applied on the angular separation between leptons, a
J/\psi
veto, the lepton transverse impact-parameter significance (
|d_{0}|/\sigma(d_{0})
), and the quality of the four-lepton vertex. The four leptons are required to be fitted to a common vertex and to be compatible with originating from the same interaction point. In addition, track- and calorimeter-based isolation requirements are applied on the lepton candidates in the pairs to suppress the non-prompt contributions. Those requirements are detailed in Ref.
[
20
]
. The full set of requirements is summarised in Table
1
.

If more than one quadruplet in an event satisfies all selection criteria, the candidates containing the leading lepton pair closest to the
Z
-boson mass are selected. If more than one quadruplet with the same leading lepton pair but different subleading pairs satisfy this criteria, the one corresponding to the lepton-flavour combination with the highest total selection efficiency is chosen, which is about 28%, 23%, 14%, and 12% for the
4\mu
,
2e2\mu
,
2\mu 2e
, and
4e
, final states, respectively. The
2e2\mu
and
2\mu 2e
final states are distinguished by the flavour of the leading lepton pair.

If after this selection, an additional lepton, not used in the quadruplet, with
p_{\text{T}}>12\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
is present in the event, it may originate from leptonic decays of
VH
and
t\bar{t}H
production-modes. To improve the mass resolution in this scenario, a decay-side ME discriminant is calculated at leading-order using
MadGraph5
for each quadruplet in the event, with the reconstructed lepton four-momenta used as inputs
[
8
]
, and the quadruplet with the highest ME value is selected. The improvement in correctly identifying the leptons in the quadruplet is about 50% for these cases.

The selected Higgs boson candidates are further refined by accounting for final-state radiation (FSR) photons, using the procedure described in Refs.
[
8
,
9
]
. For electrons, the reconstruction already includes the recovery of nearby FSR photons within
\Delta R<0.15

[
124
]
; therefore, only the recovery of far FSR photons is applied to electron candidates. For muons, both nearby and far FSR photons are explicitly recovered.

5
Background estimation

5.1
Background process with prompt leptons

The dominant background contribution arises from continuum
ZZ^{*}
production initiated by quarks or gluons. Unlike the Higgs boson signal, the
m_{4\ell}
spectrum of the
ZZ^{*}
background does not exhibit a resonant structure near the Higgs mass,
m_{H}
. This feature is exploited to constrain the normalisation of the
ZZ^{*}
background directly from data.

The shapes of the
m_{4\ell}
templates and the differential distributions for the
ZZ^{*}
background are taken from the simulation described in Section
3
. The relative contributions of the individual
ZZ^{*}
sub-processes are fixed to their simulated expectations, while the overall normalisation is left free in the statistical model through one or more scale factors defined relative to the MC-predicted yields. The binning of these scale factors differs between the two measurements and is described below.

The
ZZ^{*}
normalisation is constrained using signal-depleted sideband regions defined by
105<m_{4\ell}<115\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
130<m_{4\ell}<160\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. These regions are representative of the continuum background process under the Higgs boson peak and avoid the resonant
Z\to 4\ell
process before 105
GeV
and the fully resonant
ZZ
production after 160
GeV
.

For the fiducial cross-section measurements, including differential observables, the full fit is performed over the full mass range
105<m_{4\ell}<160\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. For differential measurements, the
ZZ^{*}
normalisation is determined independently in each bin of the measured observable. To reduce the impact of statistical fluctuations in categories with low expected event yields, neighbouring bins are combined when extracting the corresponding normalisation parameters. For the production-mode cross-section measurements in the STXS framework, the low and high mass sideband regions are merged together in
m_{4\ell}
but are further categorised according to the reconstructed jet multiplicity (
N_{\mathrm{jets}}=0
,
1
, and
\geq 2
) to reduce theoretical assumption on extrapolating results between different regions. Signal-region categories with the same jet multiplicity are fitted simultaneously with their corresponding sideband regions, using a common
ZZ^{*}
normalisation parameter for each jet-multiplicity category.

This strategy substantially reduces theoretical uncertainties in the
ZZ^{*}
background prediction for both measurements, as the statistical model primarily relies on data to constrain the normalisations, leaving only the relative normalisation between the signal and sideband regions and the modelling of the differential shapes dependent on theoretical assumptions.

Top-quark-associated background processes are treated differently in the two measurement strategies. In the fiducial and differential cross-section measurements, their contribution is small and is therefore taken directly from simulation. In the production-mode cross-section measurements analysis, where sensitivity to
t\bar{t}H
production is enhanced, a dedicated control region is included in the fit to constrain the normalisation of these processes. A single normalisation parameter is determined in a simultaneous fit to the
t\bar{t}H
signal regions and the control region, defined following the Run 2 analysis strategy described in Ref.
[
8
]
. In this control region, the upper bound of the
m_{4\ell}
selection is extended to 350
GeV
to improve the statistical precision of the constraint, while avoiding the fully resonant top-associated production.

Contributions from triboson production are taken directly from simulation in all regions considered.

5.2
Background process with non-prompt leptons

A small contribution to the selected
4\ell
sample arises from events with two or three prompt leptons accompanied by additional non-prompt leptons from heavy-flavour hadron decays, light-jet misidentification, or photon conversions (including final-state radiation), primarily from
Z
+jets,
t\bar{t}
, and
WZ
production. Non-prompt muons typically originate predominantly from heavy-flavour hadron decays, while non-prompt electrons originate from light-jet misidentification and photon conversions.
Such non-prompt leptons are typically produced around or nearby hadronic activity and therefore have a higher probability to fail the isolation and/or impact-parameter requirements used in the signal selection. This background is referred to as
reducible
and is estimated with a data-driven fake-factor method, following approaches used in other four-lepton final-state analyses
[
125
,
126
]
.

The method is based on two nested lepton definitions:
baseline
leptons satisfy the full signal selection described in Section
4
, except for the isolation and impact-parameter requirements, while
tight
leptons additionally satisfy these requirements. The fake efficiency
f
is defined as the probability for a non-prompt (fake) baseline lepton to also satisfy the tight requirements, and is measured in data after subtracting the expected prompt-lepton contamination.
The fake factor (F) is then defined as

F=\frac{f}{1-f},

(1)

relating the yield of events with tight fake leptons to the yield of events in which the corresponding leptons fail to satisfy the tight requirements.

The fake factors are measured in two control regions enriched in reducible background: a
Z
+jets-dominated region (CR
Z
) and a
t\bar{t}
-dominated region (CR
{}_{\text{top}}
). In both regions, a tight lepton pair is required together with an additional baseline lepton. In CR
Z
, the tight pair is required to be same-flavour opposite-sign with an invariant mass close to
m_{Z}
, while in CR
{}_{\text{top}}
the tight pair is required to be an
e\mu
pair and the event must contain at least one
b
-tagged jet using the working point with 85% efficiency.
Prompt-lepton contamination in the measurement regions is dominated by
WZ
production. Its contribution is estimated in each CR applying a scale factor (SF) to correct the
WZ
MC estimate with data. This SF is extracted from a dedicated region (CR
WZ
) that requires a SFOC tight lepton pair with an invariant mass close to
m_{Z}
and one extra tight lepton.

The fake factors are parameterised separately for electrons and muons in a three-dimensional map of the lepton
p_{\text{T}}
,
|\eta|
, and jet multiplicity. The final fake efficiency in the signal region (SR) is obtained by combining the measurements from CR
Z
and CR
{}_{\text{top}}
with equal weights,

f^{\text{SR}}=0.5\,f^{\text{CR}_{Z}}+0.5\,f^{\text{CR}_{\text{top}}},

(2)

while for the 0-jet category
f^{\text{SR}}_{0j}=f^{\text{CR}_{Z}}_{0j}
, since CR
{}_{\text{top}}
definition requires the presence of
b
-tagged jets. While the compositions for the non-prompt lepton in the signal region are more similar to those observed in CR
{}_{\text{top}}
, an equal-weighted average is used to ensure a robust estimate as weighting according to the composition increases sensitivity to statistical variation from CR
{}_{\text{top}}
, which has significantly lower event yields than CR
Z
. However, as described in Section
7
, the full difference between two regions is included as a conservative systematic uncertainty to account for the composition difference. This systematic uncertainty has a negligible impact on the final results.

The reducible-background yield in the signal region is estimated by applying fake-factor event weights to data events that satisfy the nominal quadruplet selection but contain at least one baseline lepton failing to meet the tight requirements, after subtracting residual prompt contributions from simulation.

The procedure is validated in two additional regions that are kinematically close to the signal region but enriched in the reducible background: a same-charge region requiring the leptons of the subleading pair to have the same charge, and an
e\mu
region requiring one lepton pair to be an
e\mu
pair, with all other selections identical to the signal region.
To reduce statistical fluctuations, a smoothing procedure is applied and the corresponding statistical uncertainty is evaluated using pseudo-datasets.

6
Analysis overview

The analysis performs two complementary measurements of the Higgs boson cross-section in the
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
decay channel. In the first approach, inclusive and differential cross-sections are measured in a fiducial phase space designed to closely mirror the detector-level selection, thereby minimising extrapolation and reducing the dependence on theoretical assumptions. In this measurement, the relative contributions of the different Higgs boson production-modes are fixed to the Standard Model expectation when deriving acceptance and migration corrections. Signal yields are extracted from maximum-likelihood fits to the reconstructed
m_{4\ell}
spectrum, and detector effects are corrected using a likelihood-based matrix unfolding procedure
[
9
]
to obtain particle-level results that are as model independent as possible and sensitive to deviations from the Standard Model.

The second, complementary approach aims to measure the underlying Higgs boson production mechanisms. Production-mode cross-sections are measured using event categories and multivariate discriminants optimised for separation of
\mathrm{ggF}
,
\mathrm{VBF}
,
VH
, and
t\bar{t}H
contributions, and are interpreted within the STXS framework. The STXS approach provides a common set of phase-space regions that enables increasingly granular measurements,
enhancing sensitivity to differences between production kinematics and potential BSM effects.

The following sections describe these two strategies in detail, first presenting the fiducial inclusive and differential cross-section measurement and then the production-mode measurement in the STXS framework. Although both approaches provide an inclusive Higgs boson production cross-section, they do so using complementary combinations and are impacted by different model dependences. In the fiducial analysis, the inclusive cross-section is obtained by combining measurements performed separately in the four decay final states, and depends on the modelling of the relative effects between the
4\mu
,
2e2\mu
,
2\mu 2e
, and
4e
channels. In the STXS measurement, the inclusive cross-section is obtained by summing the fitted production-mode contributions across the reconstructed categories, and is sensitive to the modelling of the relative signal composition across jet-multiplicity regions and categories.

6.1
Inclusive and differential cross-sections

6.1.1
Fiducial phase space and observables

The fiducial cross-sections are defined at particle level in a phase space chosen to closely match the detector-level event selection, thereby reducing the size of model-dependent acceptance extrapolations and minimising theoretical and experimental uncertainties associated with the unfolding procedure
[
9
]
. The fiducial phase space definition is outlined in Table
2
. Leptons are defined as final-state electrons and muons that do not originate from hadron or
\tau
-lepton decays, after FSR
[
127
]
. Leptons are “dressed” by adding the four-momenta of photons within a cone of
\Delta R=0.1
around the lepton direction (excluding photons from hadron decays) to the lepton four-momentum
[
9
]
to recover energy lost to FSR. Dressed leptons are required to satisfy
|\eta|<2.7
and
p_{\text{T}}>5\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
irrespective of flavour to simplify comparisons with theoretical predictions. Particle-level jets are reconstructed from final state neutral and charged particles using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm with radius parameter
R=0.4
and are required to satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>30\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
|y|<4.4
. Overlapping jets are removed if
\Delta R(\ell,\mathrm{jet})<0.1
with any selected lepton
[
9
]
. Higgs boson candidates are formed from SFOC lepton pairs using the quadruplet selection described in Section
4.2
, with the exception of the isolation, impact-parameter, and vertex requirements. Events enter the fiducial phase space if a candidate satisfying the mass and kinematic requirements is reconstructed, including
105<m_{4\ell}<160\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}

[
9
]
.

Table 2:

)

)

and 12

GeV

Fiducial production cross-sections are measured inclusively and separately for each
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
final state (
4\mu
,
2e2\mu
,
2\mu 2e
,
4e
). Differential fiducial cross-sections are measured for eight observables sensitive to the production and decay kinematics of the Higgs boson. The transverse momentum of the four-lepton system,
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
, provides a stringent test of perturbative QCD calculations and is sensitive to modifications of Higgs boson production dynamics. The rapidity of the four-lepton system,
|y_{4\ell}|
, probes the parton distribution functions of the colliding protons and is also affected by QCD radiative corrections. The invariant masses of the leading and subleading SFOC lepton pairs,
m_{12}
and
m_{34}
, are sensitive to higher-order electroweak effects and to the decay kinematics of
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
. The decay angle between the two Z bosons
\phi
, is sensitive to the Higgs boson spin and parity and to BSM contributions in the
HZZ
vertex. Jet activity is characterised by the jet multiplicity,
N_{\text{jets}}
, which is sensitive to QCD radiation and to the relative contributions of different production mechanisms. For events with at least two jets, the azimuthal separation of the two leading jets,
|\Delta\phi_{jj}|
, provides additional sensitivity to production dynamics through the structure of associated QCD radiation. In addition, a double-differential measurement of
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
versus
m_{34}
is performed, which enhances sensitivity to BSM couplings effects affecting both the production and decay of the Higgs boson.

6.1.2
Signal extraction and unfolding

The fiducial cross-section
\sigma_{j}^{\textrm{fid}}
in each bin
j
of a differential distribution (or, for the inclusive measurement, in each decay final state) is obtained from a binned maximum-likelihood fit of signal and background templates to the
m_{4\ell}
spectrum. The fit is performed simultaneously over all the reconstruction bins
i
of the corresponding observable. The expected number of events in reconstruction bin
i
, expressed as a function of
m_{4\ell}
, is given by

\begin{split}N_{i}\left(m_{4\ell}\right)=\sum_{j}{\sigma_{j}^{\textrm{fid}}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{i}\left(m_{4\ell}\right)\cdot r_{ij}\cdot\left(1+f_{i}^{\textrm{nonfid}}\right)\cdot\mathcal{L}+N_{i}^{\textrm{bkg}}\left(m_{4\ell}\right)}\end{split}

(3)

where
\sigma_{j}^{\textrm{fid}}=\sigma_{j}\cdot a_{j}\cdot\mathcal{B}
. Here,
\sigma_{j}
is the total production cross-section in particle-level bin
j
,
a_{j}
is the particle-level acceptance of the fiducial selection in that bin, and
\mathcal{B}
is the branching ratio. The integrated luminosity is denoted by
\mathcal{L}
, and
N_{i}^{\textrm{bkg}}(m_{4\ell})
is the background contribution in reconstruction bin
i
.
The term
\mathcal{P}_{i}(m_{4\ell})
is the signal
m_{4\ell}
template for reconstruction bin
i
taken from simulation. The response matrix element
r_{ij}
corresponds to the probability that an event generated in fiducial bin
j
is reconstructed in bin
i
. The fiducial leakage factor,
f_{i}^{\textrm{nonfid}}
, accounts for the fraction of events produced outside the fiducial phase space that are still reconstructed within the detector-level signal region. The factors
a_{j}
,
r_{ij}
, and
f_{i}^{\textrm{nonfid}}
are derived from simulated signal samples, assuming Standard Model production-mode fractions within their associated theoretical uncertainties.

As outlined in Section
5
, the background contribution
N_{i}^{\textrm{bkg}}(m_{4\ell})
includes the continuum
ZZ^{*}
component and smaller prompt and non-prompt backgrounds. To minimise theory dependence, the overall
ZZ^{*}
yield is constrained directly from data by including
m_{4\ell}
sideband regions in the likelihood fit. The remaining prompt and non-prompt contributions are taken from simulation and the data-driven method respectively.

The binning choice mostly follows the previous measurement
[
9
]
, which was designed to ensure an expected statistical significance per bin of more than two standard deviations for the SM signal process and to minimise migrations between bins. These same criteria are used to define the binning of the new observable
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
versus
m_{34}
. In most cases, the bin width is more than twice the experimental resolution. The response matrices for all the variables considered are well-conditioned and therefore no regularisation of the unfolding is applied.

The response matrix for
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{H}
and
N_{\mathrm{jets}}
are shown in Figure
1
. The bin-to-bin migrations are quantified by the
purity
, defined as the fraction of reconstructed events in a given bin that originate from the corresponding particle-level bin. The purity is mostly larger than 80% for the observables related to the Higgs decay kinematics, the
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
,
y_{4\ell}
,
m_{12}
,
m_{34}
,
\phi
and
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
versus
m_{34}
. Instead, for the jet-related observables,
N_{\text{jets}}
and
|\Delta\phi_{jj}|
, migrations are larger due to the jet energy resolution and residual pile-up effects, reducing for example the purity in the highest jet-multiplicity bin to 66%. The fiducial leakage
mostly ranges from 1% to 2%, depending on the bin of the unfolded observable or the decay final state. Only the first bin of
m_{12}
and the last bin of
m_{34}
observables reach values of 4% and 6% respectively.

((a))
p_{T}^{4\ell}

((b))
N_{\text{jets}}

Figure 1:
Response matrices, derived from simulation, for (a) the transverse momentum of the four-lepton system
p_{\textrm{T}}^{4\ell}
and (b) the jet multiplicity
N_{\text{jets}}
. The response matrix is used in the unfolding procedure (Eq.
3
). The elements
r_{ij}
correspond to the probability that an event generated in fiducial bin
j
is reconstructed in bin
i
. Only reconstructed events that are matched to particle-level (“truth”) events are included. Bins with values below 0.005 are omitted for clarity.

6.2
Production-mode cross-sections

The cross-sections are also probed separately for four different Higgs boson production-modes: gluon–gluon fusion (
\mathrm{ggF}
), vector-boson fusion (
\mathrm{VBF}
) and associated production with vector bosons (
VH
, where
V
=
W
or
Z
) or top quark pairs (
t\bar{t}H
).

6.2.1
Simplified template cross-sections

In the STXS framework
[
128
,
22
,
23
]
, Higgs boson production cross-sections are measured in exclusive particle-level regions (production bins) designed to minimise the impact of theoretical uncertainties that would otherwise enter through acceptance extrapolations, while retaining sensitivity to production dynamics and potential BSM effects. Two alternative STXS binnings are considered: a production-mode (also referred to as Stage-0) measurement, in which cross-sections are extracted for the four dominant production-modes (
\mathrm{ggF}
,
\mathrm{VBF}
,
VH
, and
t\bar{t}H
), and a Reduced Stage-1.2 measurement that provides additional kinematic information by subdividing production according to jet activity and Higgs boson kinematics. Bins are merged relative to the full binning where required by the statistical precision. All production bins are defined for Higgs bosons with rapidity
|y_{H}|<2.5
, and no requirements are placed on particle-level leptons. Particle-level jets are used for this categorisation and are built using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm with
R=0.4
from stable particles (lifetime
c\tau>10
mm) and are required to satisfy
p_{\text{T}}>30\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
with no rapidity restriction; Higgs decay products and leptons/neutrinos from leptonic
V
decays are excluded from the jet clustering, while hadronic
V
decay products are included
[
23
]
.

In the production-mode measurement, contributions from
b\bar{b}H
are included in the
\mathrm{ggF}
bin and contributions from
tH
are included in the
t\bar{t}H
bin, with their relative fractions fixed to SM predictions.
VH
events with hadronic
V
decays are retained in a dedicated
VH
bin
[
22
]
. For the Reduced Stage-1.2 measurement, the
\mathrm{ggF}
and
gg\to ZH
(hadronic
Z
) contributions are split by
p_{\text{T}}^{H}
and jet multiplicity, including a dedicated high-
p_{\text{T}}^{H}
bin with
p_{\text{T}}^{H}\geq 200\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
to enhance sensitivity to BSM effects. The electroweak
qqH
contribution (including
\mathrm{VBF}
and hadronic
VH
) is further partitioned using
p_{\text{T}}^{H}
and the dijet invariant mass
m_{jj}
, defining a BSM-sensitive region with
p_{\text{T}}^{H}\geq 200\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
m_{jj}>350\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, a
VH
-like region with
60<m_{jj}<120\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, and a remaining region targeting
\mathrm{VBF}
, while
VH
with leptonic
V
decays is treated separately as
VH
-Lep. The
t\bar{t}H
production bin definition is unchanged with respect to the production-mode interpretation.

The exact definitions of the STXS production bins and the corresponding labels used in this analysis are shown on the left grayed panel of Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
Two sets of exclusive phase-space regions (production bins) are defined at particle level for the measurement of the Higgs boson production cross-sections: the production-mode Stage 0 and the Reduced Stage 1.2 scheme (left and middle-left shaded panels). The corresponding reconstructed event categories are shown for signal (middle-right panel) and sidebands (right panel). The description of the production bins is given in Section
6.2.1
, while the reconstructed signal-region and sideband event categories are described in Section
6.2.2
. The
b\bar{b}H
(
tH
) contribution is included in the
\mathrm{ggF}
(
ttH
) production bins. The colours of each reconstructed event-category box indicate the relevant production processes that contribute to the reconstructed category. The reconstructed signal categories are defined sequentially from bottom to top, such that each event is assigned to the first category whose selection criteria are satisfied.

6.2.2
Event categorisation

Signal-region events with a reconstructed Higgs boson candidate in
115<m_{4\ell}<130\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
are assigned to mutually exclusive categories sensitive to different production bins in the STXS framework, in a sequential procedure, following the ordering shown in Figure
2
. Events are first tested against the
ttH
-enriched selection, which targets
t\bar{t}H
production using requirements on additional leptons and/or high jet and
b
-tag multiplicities. In particular, events are selected if they contain at least one additional isolated lepton with
p_{\text{T}}>12\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and satisfy at least one of the following: at least two
b
-tagged jets at the 85% working point, at least five jets with at least one
b
-tagged jet at the 85% working point, or at least two jets with at least one
b
-tagged jet at the 65% working point. Events without an additional isolated lepton can also enter the
ttH
-enriched selection if they satisfy tighter jet and
b
-tag requirements, namely at least five jets with at least two
b
-tagged jets at the 85% working point, or at least four jets with at least one
b
-tagged jet at the 65% working point.

Events with additional isolated leptons that do not satisfy the
ttH
-enriched selection define the
VH
category targeting leptonic vector-boson decays (
VH
-Lep-enriched). The remaining events are then categorised by jet multiplicity (
N_{\mathrm{jets}}=0
, 1, or
\geq 2
) and further subdivided using
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{H}
and, for events with at least two jets, the dijet invariant mass
m_{jj}
to target specific STXS production bins. Events with at least two jets are separated into a BSM-like category at high
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{H}
and large
m_{jj}
and a complementary category that contains the bulk of the
\mathrm{VBF}
/
VH
contributions in addition to
\mathrm{ggF}
. Both the 0-jet and 1-jet regions are further split into low-, medium-, and high-
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{H}
intervals.

Sideband categories in
m_{4\ell}
are defined to constrain the dominant non-resonant
ZZ^{*}
background in different jet multiplicity regions (SB-
0j
, SB-
1j
, and SB-
2j
) and the top-quark-associated
tXX
background (SB-
tXX
-enriched) as described in Section
5
.

The definitions of the reconstruction categories and the corresponding names used in this analysis are shown on the right panel of Figure
2
. The composition of production-modes per reconstructed event category of these categories is shown in Figure
3
.

Figure 3:
Standard Model signal composition in terms of the Reduced Stage-1.2 production bins in each reconstructed event category. The
b\bar{b}H
and
tH
contributions are included in the
\mathrm{ggF}
and
t\bar{t}H
production bins, respectively.

6.2.3
Multivariate discriminants and Signal Extraction

In the STXS measurement, several Higgs boson production mechanisms contribute to each reconstructed event category, together with multiple background processes. To enhance the separation power beyond the cut-based categorisation, multivariate discriminants are constructed using neural networks (NNs) based on a Set Transformer architecture
[
129
]
.
The networks are trained independently in each reconstructed category on simulated samples of the relevant Higgs-boson production modes and dominant non-Higgs-boson backgrounds, and are optimised to classify events according to their most likely signal-production or background process.
The NN inputs are organised into event-level global observables and sets of reconstructed physics objects. Lepton and jet information is provided as variable-length sets to Set Transformer blocks, which provide permutation-invariant processing through attention mechanisms
[
24
]
, while global variables are combined with the learned set representations in a classifier head. Each of the NN output nodes is labelled as NN
P
, where
P
refers to one or multiple targeted processes, and is related to the probability of an event originating from that process.
The specific feature choices, the corresponding training targets and the output nodes follow the strategy outlined in Table
3
.
Good agreement between data and simulation is observed for the input variables used in the NN training, as illustrated in Figure
10
. In addition, validation studies are performed in
ZZ^{*}
-enriched control regions to verify that the NN outputs are well modelled and that no overtraining or simulation-specific features are learned.
In categories with limited statistical power, no NN discriminant is applied and only the observed yield enters the fit.

The STXS cross-sections are obtained from a simultaneous binned likelihood fit to the discriminant distributions across all reconstructed signal categories defined in Section
6.2.2
, with the dominant background processes constrained as described in Section
5
. The parameters of interest are collected in a vector
\vec{\sigma}
and are related to the reconstructed event yields
N_{ik}
in a histogram bin
k
of the discriminating observable in reconstructed category
i
via

\begin{split}N_{ik}=\vec{A}_{ik}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\cdot\mathcal{B}\cdot\mathcal{L}+N^{\textrm{bkg}}_{ik}\,,\end{split}

(4)

where
\mathcal{B}
is the
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
branching ratio,
\mathcal{L}
is the integrated luminosity, and
N^{\textrm{bkg}}_{ik}
is the expected background contribution. The vector
\vec{A}_{ik}
contains the signal selection factors that map particle-level STXS bins to reconstructed categories and discriminant bins, and is determined from MC simulation. In the Stage-0 interpretation, the components of
\vec{\sigma}
(and thus of
\vec{A}_{ik}
) correspond to the chosen set of production-mode cross-sections, while in the Reduced Stage-1.2 interpretation they correspond to bins defined in Section
6.2.1
. In both cases, each factor
A_{ik}^{j}
is defined as the fraction of generated signal events in STXS bin
j
that satisfy the reconstruction and event selection and populate histogram bin
k
of the discriminant observable in reconstructed category
i
.

Table 3:
Input variables used for the neural-network trainings in the reconstructed STXS categories that use binned NN discriminants. The inputs are grouped into event-level

variables and object-level variables provided as variable-length

[

.

6.3
Statistical method

All measurements are performed with a binned profile-likelihood-ratio fit
[
132
]
. The likelihood is built as product of Poisson terms for the fitted discriminant distributions in each differential bin or STXS category, and includes nuisance parameters that account for experimental and theoretical systematic uncertainties affecting the normalisation and shapes of the signal and background templates (Section
7
). The parameters of interest are the cross-sections and the background normalisation factor introduced in Sections
6.1
and
6.2
and are extracted by minimising
-2\ln\lambda
, where
\lambda
is the profile likelihood ratio. The asymptotic approximation
[
132
]
is validated with pseudo-experiments; where fewer than three events are observed in a bin, the quoted uncertainties are corrected to those obtained from pseudo-experiments.

The compatibility of a measured cross-section and its theoretical prediction is tested by computing a
p
-value based on the difference between the value of
-2\ln\lambda
at the best-fit value and the value obtained by fixing the cross-section in each bin to that predicted by SM theory. These
p
-values do not include the uncertainties in the theoretical predictions.

7
Systematic uncertainties

The dominant uncertainty in the measurement arises from statistical uncertainty.
In addition, many systematic uncertainties are considered.
Experimental uncertainties arise from object reconstruction, identification, isolation, energy and momentum scale, resolution, and trigger efficiencies, the determination of the integrated luminosity and the data-driven background estimate.
Additional theoretical uncertainties are related to the modelling of the signal and background processes.

7.1
Experimental uncertainties

The uncertainty in the integrated luminosity is
1.9
%
[
31
]
. This affects the signal and background yields predicted by simulation if no normalisation to data is used. The uncertainty due to pile-up modelling are below 2% in the entire
m_{4\ell}
range considered.

The reconstruction and identification efficiencies for muons and electrons are determined using the methods described in Refs.
[
115
,
112
]
. For muons, these have associated uncertainties between
0.1\%
and
1.3\%
. For electrons with
p_{\text{T}}<15\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
conservative uncertainties up to
17\%
are included to cover the change in detector conditions between Runs 2 and 3 and updates to the simulation.
The isolation efficiency uncertainty is approximately
1\%
for electrons and
0.5-2\%
for muons.

Lepton energy and momentum scale and resolution are corrected to describe the data using the procedures documented in Refs.
[
114
,
116
]
, and the associated uncertainties have an impact at the most of
1-1.5\%
on the measured cross-sections.

Jet energy scale and resolution are calibrated using the procedure described in Ref.
[
119
]
. An additional uncertainty is assigned to jets in the kinematic region of
|\eta|>3.2
to account for an observed mismodelling on the jet energy calibration in the forward calorimeters.

Uncertainties in the
b
-jet identification efficiency are taken from 2022 and 2023 data and applied also to 2024 data and conservatively increased by ten times to reflect possible differences between different data-taking periods. This is done only in the
t\bar{t}H
category and
tXX
sideband, where
b
-jets are required and the impact is at the level of 1%.

Uncertainties in the lepton trigger efficiency have a negligible impact on the analysis, as does the uncertainty on the measured Higgs boson mass of
m_{H}=125.09\pm 0.24\penalty\ \,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}

[
14
]
.

For the data-driven measurement of the reducible background, several sources of uncertainty are considered. The dominant contribution arises from the statistical uncertainty in the region containing a quadruplet with at least one baseline lepton failing to meet the tight requirements, where the fake factors are applied. An additional significant contribution comes from the systematic uncertainty associated with the choice of control regions used to derive the fake factors. This uncertainty reflects the variation of the result when the fake factors are extracted using only CR
Z
or only CR
{}_{\text{top}}
, instead of their nominal combination, as described in Section
5
. Those uncertainties have an impact that ranges from 1% to 9% both in the differential and in the production cross-section measurements.
The statistical uncertainty due to the limited statistics in the CRs is also propagated to the fake factor estimate and has a negligible impact on the final yields. Finally, the uncertainty in the
WZ
is estimated by varying the fitted normalization within its statistical error and has negligible impact on the estimate.

7.2
Theoretical uncertainties

Uncertainties in the signal prediction due to missing higher-order QCD eﬀects are estimated by using the scheme described in Ref.
[
133
]
.
Parton shower and multiple-parton interaction modelling uncertainties on the acceptance are estimated by using a prediction based on
Herwig
7 as alternative parton shower generator and using tune eigenvector variations within the nominal parton shower generator tune, A14.
Uncertainties related to the PDF set are evaluated using 40 eigenvector variations of the PDF4LHC21 PDF set.
An additional uncertainty of the
H\rightarrow ZZ^{*}\rightarrow 4\ell
decay branching ratio of 2.2%
[
87
,
134
]
is assigned when extrapolating to the total phase-space. These theory-related uncertainties enter the measurements in different ways. These uncertainties generally have small effects on both cross-section measurements, mainly through acceptance and migration. In contrast, for the global strength measurement and interpretation of the results, the parameters of interest (
\mu
) are defined relative to the SM predictions. In this case the same modelling uncertainties propagate directly to the predicted signal yields and have a larger impact.

Uncertainties in the prediction of the
ZZ^{*}
background from missing higher-order QCD effects are taken into account by varying the factorisation and renormalisation scales in the calculation by factors of two and choosing the configuration with the largest impact. PDF-related uncertainties are evaluated using the various PDFs within the
NNPDF3.0
PDF set using the method outlined in
[
99
]
.
A further modelling uncertainty is assigned by comparing the prediction from the nominal
Sherpa
sample to a simulation in
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
matched to
Pythia
8 with the
FxFx
prescription
[
50
,
44
]
.
As this background contribution is normalised to data, only the change in differential shapes impacts the measurements (
1-3\%
), while the effect in the shapes of the NN discriminants and in the relative contribution of this background between the sidebands and the signal regions for the STXS measurement is negligible. For the tXX process, uncertainties due to PDF and missing higher-order QCD effects are estimated from simulation using a similar procedure as the
ZZ^{*}
process.

Finally, unfolding-related systematic uncertainties for the inclusive and differential cross-section measurements arise from uncertainties in the production-mode composition that affect the response matrices. Additional uncertainties come from a potential bias introduced by the unfolding method. This bias is evaluated independently in each bin by comparing the unfolded cross-section in simulation with the expected result when the underlying true cross-sections of the simulated sample are varied within their expected statistical uncertainty. The impact of these uncertainties is negligible.

<m_{4\ell}<

are presented in Table

8.1
Inclusive fiducial and total cross-sections

The inclusive fiducial and total cross-sections of the
H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell
process are measured by combining the results from the four decay final states assuming the relative SM branching ratio and are summarised in Figure
5
. A normalisation factor of
N/N_{\mathrm{MC}}=0.94\pm 0.08
is obtained for the
ZZ^{*}
background component. The fiducial cross-section is measured as

\sigma_{\textrm{fid}}\equiv\sigma\cdot\mathcal{B}(H\to ZZ^{*}\to 4\ell)=3.65\pm 0.30(\textrm{stat.})\phantom{I}^{+0.18}_{-0.16}(\textrm{sys.})\penalty\ \textrm{fb}=3.65^{+0.35}_{-0.33}\penalty\ \textrm{fb},

consistent with the SM prediction of
\sigma_{\textrm{fid}}^{\textrm{SM}}=3.68\pm 0.17\penalty\ \textrm{fb}
. The resulting total cross-section is

\sigma_{\textrm{total}}=59.3\pm 4.8(\textrm{stat.})\pm 3.1(\textrm{sys.})\penalty\ \textrm{pb}=59.3\pm 5.7\penalty\ \textrm{pb},

consistent with the SM value of
\sigma^{\mathrm{SM}}_{\mathrm{total}}=59.9\pm 3.0
pb. The systematic uncertainty in the measurement is dominated by lepton reconstruction and isolation efficiencies. Larger uncertainties in decay channels with electrons in the subleading lepton pair reflect the conservative estimates of uncertainties on electron reconstruction efficiency. In combination with other final states, the impact of this systematic uncertainty is reduced in the inclusive fiducial measurement.
The probability of compatibility of the measured fiducial cross-section and the SM expectation is 92% and for the individual channels it ranges from 75% for
2\mu 2e
to 90% for
4e
final states.

Figure 5:
Measured fiducial and total cross-sections: (left panel) fiducial cross-sections for the four individual final states; (middle panel) inclusive fiducial cross-section obtained by combining same-flavour, different-flavour, and all final states assuming relative SM branching ratios; (right panel) total cross-section obtained after accounting for the branching ratio of the Higgs boson into four leptons and the fiducial acceptance as determined from the simulated SM Higgs boson samples. The measurement in data is shown as black circular markers with error bands indicating the total error and boxes indicating the systematic uncertainty. The measurements are compared to theoretical predictions with their associated uncertainties.

, while for MG5 are estimated from varying factorisation and renormalisation scales. The markers denoting the simulation are horizontally displaced relative to the measurement in data to improve readability. MC-based predictions for all other Higgs boson production-modes
XH
are normalised to the SM predictions. The error bars on the data points show the total uncertainties, while the systematic uncertainties are indicated by the boxes. The middle panel shows the ratio of different predictions to the data, and the grey shaded area represents the total uncertainty of the measurement. The bottom panel shows the ratios of the fitted values of the
ZZ^{*}
normalisation factors to the predictions from MC simulation.

The observed values of
\sigma_{|y_{H}|<2.5}\cdot\mathcal{B}\left(H\rightarrow ZZ^{\ast}\right)
in the Higgs boson rapidity range of
|y_{H}|<2.5
, the corresponding expected SM cross-sections, and their ratios are shown in Figure
13
for the inclusive production and for the individual production-modes as defined in Section
6.2
. Similarly, the results for the Reduced Stage 1.2 bins are shown in Figure
14
. The branching ratio for the
ZZ^{\ast}\rightarrow 4\ell
decay is fixed to the SM prediction, such that
\mathcal{B}\left(H\rightarrow ZZ^{\ast}\right)=2.62\times 10^{-2}

[
22
]
. In the ratio calculation, uncertainties on the SM expectation are presented separately and not included in the systematics. An inclusive cross-section of

\sigma_{|y_{H}|<2.5}\cdot\mathcal{B}\left(H\rightarrow ZZ^{\ast}\right)=1.42\pm 0.11(\textrm{stat.})\pm 0.12(\textrm{sys.})\penalty\ \textrm{pb}=1.42\pm 0.16\penalty\ \textrm{pb}

is measured, in agreement with the SM prediction of
\sigma_{|y_{H}|<2.5}^{\textrm{SM}}\cdot\mathcal{B}^{\textrm{SM}}\left(H\rightarrow ZZ^{\ast}\right)=1.44\pm 0.08\penalty\ \textrm{pb}
. Normalisation factors for the
ZZ^{*}
continuum background of
N_{ZZ}^{\textrm{SB}-0j}/N_{\textrm{MC}}=0.82\pm 0.14
,
N_{ZZ}^{\textrm{SB}-1j}/N_{\textrm{MC}}=1.18^{+0.22}_{-0.19}
, and
N_{ZZ}^{\textrm{SB}-2j}/N_{\textrm{MC}}=1.53^{+0.46}_{-0.37}
are obtained from the sidebands, separately for the three event categories with different jet multiplicities, while the one for the
tXX
backgrounds from the specific category
N_{tXX}^{\textrm{SB}-tXX}/N_{\textrm{MC}}=2.5^{+0.9}_{-0.7}
.

The data are also interpreted in terms of the global signal strength, where the theoretical uncertainties in the SM cross-section predictions are included in the measurement, yielding

\mu=0.99\pm 0.08(\textrm{stat.})\pm 0.10(\textrm{sys.})=0.99\pm 0.13

The dominant systematic uncertainty in inclusive cross-section measurement is the experimental uncertainty in the lepton efficiency and the integrated luminosity measurements. For the global strength measurement theoretical uncertainties in the
\mathrm{ggF}
prediction are dominant.

The dominant contribution to the measurement uncertainty in the
\mathrm{ggF}
production-mode originates from the same sources as in the inclusive measurement and in the Reduced Stage 1.2 bins with zero jets. For the
\mathrm{VBF}
production bin, the theoretical systematic uncertainties related to missing higher-order QCD corrections for both
\mathrm{VBF}
and
\mathrm{ggF}
predictions and the experimental one on the jet energy scale and resolution uncertainties become relevant. For
VH
and
t\bar{t}H
production-modes, theoretical uncertainties in the
\mathrm{ggF}
prediction and those experiment ones on jet flavour tagging are dominant. For the Reduced Stage 1.2 bins with higher jet multiplicity, the dominant cross-section uncertainties are the jet energy scale and resolution, and missing higher-order corrections. The probability of compatibility of the measured production-mode (Reduced Stage 1.2) measurement and the SM expectation is 51% (68%).
